The **Mexican drug war** ([Spanish](26825.md): *Guerra contra el narcotráfico en México*) is an [ongoing](2346678.md) [asymmetric](46863.md)[^cite_note-RPJZI-57][^cite_note-JgtBN-58] armed conflict between the [Mexican government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_government) and various drug trafficking [syndicates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syndicate). When the [Mexican military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_military) intervened in 2006, the government's main objective was to reduce drug-related violence. The Mexican government has asserted that its primary focus is on dismantling the cartels and preventing [drug trafficking](28345164.md). The conflict has been described as the Mexican [theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater_\(warfare\)) of the global [war on drugs](1181646.md), as led by the [United States federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_federal_government).[^cite_note-59] Analysts estimate wholesale earnings from illicit drug sales range from \$13.6 to \$49.4 billion annually.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-uH1cd-61][^cite_note-spread-62]&#x20;

Although Mexican drug trafficking organizations have existed for decades, their power increased[^cite_note-5cBhN-63][^cite_note-HNU9G-64] after the demise of the Colombian [Cali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cali_Cartel) and [Medellín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medell%C3%ADn_Cartel) cartels in the 1990s, and the fragmentation of the [Guadalajara Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_Cartel) in the late 1980s. The conflict formally began with President [Felipe Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n) (2006–2012) launching [Operation Michoacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Michoac%C3%A1n) in 2006, which deployed tens of thousands of [federal troops](23384265.md) and [police](9964117.md) in a militarized campaign against the cartels initially targeted in [Michoacán](195805.md), [Ciudad Juárez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Ju%C3%A1rez), [Tijuana](101739.md), and [Tamaulipas](222254.md). However, arrests and killings of cartel leaders caused cartels to splinter into smaller, more violent factions, escalating turf wars and contributing to rising homicide rates nationwide.[^cite_note-progress-65][^cite_note-L2QWa-66][^cite_note-HzeyQ-67] By the end of Calderón's administration in 2012, the official death toll of the Mexican drug war was at least 60,000.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-68] Estimates set the death toll above 120,000 killed by 2013, not counting 27,000 missing.[^cite_note-JTx4M-69][^cite_note-F575R-70]&#x20;

Successive administrations have promised changes in strategy but have upheld the use of militarized tactics. Under President [Enrique Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto) (2012–2018), the government pledged to shift focus from high-profile arrests to de-escalation and reducing violence, but setbacks such as the prison escape of cartel leader [Joaquín "El Chapo" Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_%22El_Chapo%22_Guzm%C3%A1n) and the 2014 [Iguala mass kidnapping](44077808.md) drew international condemnation. President [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador) (2018–2024) pledged to address the social roots of crime through poverty reduction and youth programs, and declared that the war was over; however, the statement was criticized, as security policy continued to rely on the newly created [National Guard](61282251.md), which has gradually replaced the [Mexican Army](2948082.md) in policing roles. This strategy has continued under President [Claudia Sheinbaum](57813311.md) (2024–present).&#x20;

Since the beginning of the conflict, [law enforcement in Mexico](19426382.md) has been criticized for corruption, collusion with cartels, and impunity. Federal law enforcement has been reorganized at least five times since 1982. During this period, there have been at least four elite [special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_forces) created as new, corruption-free soldiers who could fight Mexico's endemic bribery system.[^cite_note-sPnCp-71] The militarization of Mexican society [has drawn criticism for human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Mexico), such as [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killings), [enforced disappearances](686148.md), targeting of journalists, and [torture](47702.md).&#x20;

## Background

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Mexican_drug_war).

Due to its location, Mexico has long been used as a staging and transshipment point for narcotics and contraband between Latin America and United States markets. Mexican [bootleggers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rum-running) supplied [alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_\(drug\)) to American gangsters throughout [Prohibition in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition_in_the_U.S.), and the onset of the [illegal drug trade](218449.md) with the U.S. began when Prohibition came to an end in 1933.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVulliamy201021–22-72] In 1940, under president [Lázaro Cárdenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A1zaro_C%C3%A1rdenas#Legalization_of_drugs) and the impulsion of Mexican psychiatrist [Leopoldo Salazar Viniegra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopoldo_Salazar_Viniegra#Legalization_of_drugs), Mexico legalized all drugs, in an early attempt to prevent the development of illegal drug trafficking organizations.[^cite_note-El_Universal-2020-73] The law was in effect for about 5 months when the Mexican government repealed it, allegedly under the increasing economic and political pressure from the U.S.[^cite_note-PerezRicart-74]&#x20;

During [World War II](32927.md), the United States experienced shortages of medical [morphine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphine) after opium supplies from Asia were disrupted by the [Pacific War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War). In response, Mexican authorities, in cooperation with U.S. officials, expanded regulated [opium poppy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium_poppy) cultivation in northwestern Mexico, including rural areas surrounding [Culiacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culiac%C3%A1n) in the state of [Sinaloa](222251.md).[^cite_note-75] Farmers in the mountainous regions of [Sierra Madre Occidental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Madre_Occidental) near [Culiacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culiac%C3%A1n), produced opium that was processed into legal morphine for wartime medical use by Allied forces. Although the program ended after 1945, the agricultural knowledge, smuggling routes, and local intermediary networks developed during the wartime period persisted. Historians and criminologists have identified these postwar networks as an early foundation for later illicit drug trafficking organizations in [Sinaloa](222251.md).[^cite_note-76]&#x20;

From the late 1960s to the early 1970s, with the rapid surge in [recreational drug use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recreational_drug_use) in the U.S., the production of narcotics in Mexico, particularly [marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marijuana), expanded exponentially, and Mexican criminals started to smuggle drugs on a major scale.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVulliamy201021–22-72][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrillo201239–40-77] During the 1960s and 1970s, Mexico participated in a series of U.S.-backed anti-narcotics initiatives, including [Operation Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Intercept) and [Operation Condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Condor). These operations were formally justified on the grounds of combating the cultivation of opium poppies and marijuana in Mexico's so-called "Golden Triangle" region, an area encompassing parts of the states of [Sinaloa](222251.md), [Durango](222234.md), and [Chihuahua](23962301.md).[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81]&#x20;

As part of the campaign, the Mexican government deployed about 10,000 soldiers and police. The operation resulted in mass arrests, torture, and imprisonment of peasants who were often accused of aiding leftist insurgency groups, but no major traffickers were captured. Contemporary assessments deemed the initiatives a failure, citing their inability to curb narcotics production, enabling military corruption, and their record of human rights abuses in rural areas.[^cite_note-MYYPC-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84]&#x20;

As U.S. efforts in the [war on drugs](1181646.md) intensified, crackdowns in Florida and the Caribbean during the [Miami drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_drug_war) forced Colombian traffickers to develop new routes for smuggling cocaine into the United States. By the early 1980s, the [Medellin Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medellin_Cartel) and [Cali Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cali_Cartel) oversaw production, while distribution increasingly relied on Mexican traffickers. Drawing on existing heroin and [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis) smuggling networks, the [Guadalajara Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_Cartel), led by [Miguel Ángel Félix Gallardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_F%C3%A9lix_Gallardo), emerged as intermediaries, transporting Colombian cocaine across the [Mexico–United States border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border).[^cite_note-k2qYe-85]&#x20;

By the mid-1980s, the Guadalajara Cartel had firmly established itself as a reliable transporter, initially paid in cash but shifting by the late 1980s to a payment-in-kind arrangement.[^cite_note-Stratfor_Intelligence-86] While many factors contributed to the escalation of drug trafficking violence, security analysts trace the origins of cartel power to the unraveling of an implicit arrangement between traffickers and then-ruling [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party) (PRI), which began to lose its grip on power in the 1980s.[^cite_note-Tet2n-87] The fighting between rival drug cartels began in earnest after the 1989 arrest of Félix Gallardo, with cartel infighting escalating in the 1990s.[^cite_note-BoKYV-88]&#x20;

## Government operations

### Vicente Fox

Main article: [Presidency of Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Vicente_Fox)

The PRI ruled Mexico for over 70 years, during which cartels grew in power and anti-drug efforts targeted the seizure of marijuana and opium crops in remote regions. In 2000, [Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fox) of the [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_\(Mexico\)) (PAN) became the first non-PRI president since 1929; his term saw declining homicide rates through 2007, and initially, broad public optimism about regime change.&#x20;

[Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas), then the armed wing of the [Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Cartel), based in [Nuevo Laredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuevo_Laredo), [Tamaulipas](222254.md), [escalated violence to unprecedented levels in the summer of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinaloa_Cartel%E2%80%93Gulf_Cartel_conflict) through gruesome violence and military-like tactics against the [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md).[^cite_note-89] Los Zetas turf conflict also instilled terror against journalists and civilians of Nuevo Laredo. This set a new precedent, which cartels later mimicked.[^cite_note-90] These activities were not widely reported by the Mexican media at the time. However, key conflicts occurred, including the Sinaloa Cartel counterattacks and the advance on the Gulf Cartel's main regions in Tamaulipas.&#x20;

It is estimated that in the first eight months of 2005, about 110 people died in Nuevo Laredo, Tamaulipas, as a result of the fighting between the Gulf and Sinaloa cartels.[^cite_note-NiSrq-91] The same year, there was another surge in violence in the state of [Michoacán](195805.md) as [La Familia Michoacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Familia_Michoacana) drug cartel established itself after splintering from its former allies, the Gulf Cartel and [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas).&#x20;

### Felipe Calderón

Main articles: [Felipe Calderón § Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n#Presidency), and [Operation Michoacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Michoac%C3%A1n)



President Felipe Calderón



Mexican soldiers training in August 2010

Following the contested [2006 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Mexican_general_election), Felipe Calderón initiated [Operation Michoacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Michoac%C3%A1n), a militarized campaign against drug cartels as an effort to consolidate political authority, strengthen the legitimacy of his administration, and rally public support.[^cite_note-iht.com-92][^cite_note-EoHlf-93] Often described as the first major campaign of the conflict, Operation Michoacán marked the beginning of large-scale confrontations between government forces and drug cartels, eventually involving about 45,000 troops together with state and federal police.[^cite_note-war-94]&#x20;

Calderón's government pioneered a militarized "kingpin strategy" that relied on [Mexican Army](2948082.md) and [Federal Police](9964117.md) deployments to capture or kill cartel leaders. This security approach was led by [Genaro García Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genaro_Garc%C3%ADa_Luna) ([Secretariat of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Public_Security)), [Eduardo Medina Mora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Medina_Mora) ([Attorney General of Mexico](2586359.md)), and [Guillermo Galván Galván](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Galv%C3%A1n_Galv%C3%A1n) ([Secretariat of National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_National_Defense)). Early operations included [Operation Baja California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Baja_California), [Operation Sinaloa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Sinaloa), and [Operation Chihuahua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Chihuahua).[^cite_note-95] Although drug-related violence spiked markedly in contested areas along the U.S. border, such as [Ciudad Juárez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Ju%C3%A1rez), [Tijuana](101739.md), and [Matamoros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matamoros,_Tamaulipas), the government was initially successful in detaining and killing high-ranking cartel members, including [Alfredo Beltrán Leyva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva), [Arturo Beltrán Leyva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva), [Ignacio Coronel Villarreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignacio_Coronel_Villarreal), [Antonio Cárdenas Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_C%C3%A1rdenas_Guill%C3%A9n), and [Vicente Carrillo Leyva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Carrillo_Leyva).[^cite_note-96] Calderón expressed that the cartels seek "to replace the government" and "are trying to impose a monopoly by force of arms, and are even trying to impose their own laws".[^cite_note-CZmux-97]&#x20;



Cooperation of the Mexican Navy in the Mexican Army transfer as well as the recognition of cultivation areas

Although Calderón's strategy intended to end violence between rival cartels, critics argue that it worsened the conflict. By removing cartel leaders through arrests or killings, his administration created leadership vacuums that sparked internal power struggles and greater competition between cartels.[^cite_note-rBpl1-98] Balance of power shifts meant that new cartels emerged as other groups weakened, for example, the fragmentation of [La Familia Michoacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Familia_Michoacana), which gave rise to the [Knights Templar Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Templar_Cartel). Splintered cartels fought to exploit overlapping patches of smuggling routes and territories, and also sought to manipulate the system by leaking intelligence to Mexican authorities or the U.S. [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md) (DEA) to turn law enforcement against their rivals, using knowledge from the groups they had broken away from.[^cite_note-Burton-99]&#x20;

During Calderón's presidential term, the murder rate of Mexico increased dramatically.[^cite_note-q15rH-100] Annual homicides rose from more than 5,000 in 2008[^cite_note-2fjyB-101] to 9,600 in 2009 and over 15,000 in 2010.[^cite_note-sLwbw-102] By the end of Calderón's presidency, his administration's statistics claimed that, during his six-year term, 50,000 drug-related homicides occurred.[^cite_note-mwLva-103] Outside sources claimed more than 120,000 murders happened in the same period as a result of Calderón's strategy.[^cite_note-tiRym-104] Some analysts, including U.S. Ambassador in Mexico [Carlos Pascual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Pascual_\(diplomat\)), argued that this increase was a direct result of Calderón's military measures.[^cite_note-google.com-105] Between 2007 and 2012, Mexico's [National Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Human_Rights_Commission_\(Mexico\)) received nearly 5,800 complaints of military abuse and issued around 90 detailed reports documenting violations against civilians committed while the armed forces carried out policing duties. The Mexican military operated with minimal accountability for abuses committed in its campaigns.[^cite_note-NXEpZ-106]&#x20;

#### Escalation (2008–12)



Mexican army fighting drug cartels in [Michoacán](195805.md)

In April 2008, General [Sergio Aponte Polito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Aponte_Polito), the man in charge of the anti-drug campaign in the state of [Baja California](199793.md), made several allegations of corruption against the police forces in the region. Among his claims, Aponte stated that he believed Baja California's anti-kidnapping squad was actually a kidnapping team working in conjunction with organized crime, and that bribed police units were used as bodyguards for drug traffickers.[^cite_note-Aponte-107] These accusations sent shock waves through the state government. Many of the more than 50 accused officials quit or fled. Four months later, Aponte was relieved of his command.[^cite_note-IYuyH-108]&#x20;

Between 2009 and 2011, [Ciudad Juárez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciudad_Ju%C3%A1rez), [Chihuahua](23962301.md) recorded the highest homicide rate in the world, with more than 200 murders per 100,000 inhabitants. Much of the violence was driven by clashes between the Sinaloa Cartel and the [Juárez Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ju%C3%A1rez_Cartel), and the resulting breakdown of public security produced a climate of pervasive lawlessness.[^cite_note-109] In March 2009, President Calderón called in an additional 5,000 Mexican Army troops to Ciudad Juárez. The [U.S. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Homeland_Security) also said that it was considering using state [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_of_the_United_States) troops to help the [U.S. Border Patrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Border_Patrol) counter the threat of drug violence in Mexico from spilling over the border into the U.S. The governors of [Arizona](21883824.md) and [Texas](29810.md) encouraged the federal government to use additional National Guard troops from their states to help those already there supporting state law enforcement efforts against drug trafficking.[^cite_note-BBC_News_–_Americas_–_March-12-09-110] In 2008, Calderón signed the [Mérida Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida_Initiative) with the United States, which provided funding, training, and intelligence, and allowed U.S. personnel to operate in Mexico in advisory and intelligence-sharing capacities.[^cite_note-\:0-111]&#x20;

By 2011, the [Mexican Armed Forces](23384265.md) had captured 11,544 people who were believed to have been involved with the cartels and organized crime.[^cite_note-9dwGW-112] In the year prior, 28,000 individuals were arrested on drug-related charges. In October 2012, [Mexican Navy](1225277.md) forces killed [Heriberto Lazcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heriberto_Lazcano), leader of Los Zetas, in a shootout in [Sabinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabinas,_Coahuila), [Coahuila](160592.md), after gunmen attacked their patrol. The operation came just hours after the capture of another senior Zeta, Salvador Alfonso Martínez Escobedo.[^cite_note-ceFJK-113][^cite_note-aljaz-114][^cite_note-Jnfav-115][^cite_note-reutersmost-116] Lazcano's death is viewed as the most significant cartel leader killing in Calderón's administration. It strengthened the Navy's standing, allowed [Miguel Treviño Morales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Trevi%C3%B1o_Morales) to take control of Los Zetas, and ultimately benefited Joaquín "El Chapo" Guzmán, whose Sinaloa Cartel sought to dominate the Nuevo Laredo smuggling routes.[^cite_note-BNmOL-117][^cite_note-longmire12-118]&#x20;

### Enrique Peña Nieto

Main article: [Enrique Peña Nieto § Presidency (2012–2018)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto#Presidency_\(2012%E2%80%932018\))



President Enrique Peña Nieto, accompanied by Cabinet members, holds a press conference in the Palacio Nacional announcing the capture of [Joaquín Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Guzm%C3%A1n)

In 2012, newly elected President [Enrique Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto), from the PRI, emphasized that he did not support the involvement of armed American agents in Mexico and was only interested in training Mexican forces in [counter-insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-insurgency) tactics.[^cite_note-NrFzg-119] At the start of his term, Peña Nieto promised to de-escalate the conflict, focusing on lowering criminal violence rates, as opposed to the previous policy of attacking drug-trafficking organizations by arresting or killing the cartel leaders and intercepting their shipments.[^cite_note-ulcZ1-120] His administration's security policy was shaped by [Miguel Ángel Osorio Chong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_Osorio_Chong) ([Secretary of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Interior_\(Mexico\))), [Jesús Murillo Karam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Murillo_Karam) (Attorney General), and [Salvador Cienfuegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Cienfuegos) (National Defense).[^cite_note-121] In the first 14 months of his administration, between December 2012 and January 2014, 23,640 people died in the conflict.[^cite_note-zetatijuana.com-122]&#x20;

During 2012 and 2013, Mexico saw the rise of *[grupos de autodefensa comunitaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupos_de_autodefensa_comunitaria)*, vigilante self-defense groups in rural communities that took up arms against criminal groups that wanted to impose dominance in their towns, entering a new phase in the Mexican drug war.[^cite_note-9RXxH-123] This strategy, encouraged by [Óscar Naranjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Naranjo_Trujillo), one of Peña Nieto's security advisors,[^cite_note-FE7Wi-124] crumbled when *autodefensas* began having internal struggles and disagreements with the government, as well as infiltration and co-optation by organized crime, causing Peña Nieto's administration to distance from them.[^cite_note-ISX0V-125]&#x20;

The 2014 [Iguala mass kidnapping](44077808.md), in which 43 students from the [Ayotzinapa Rural Teachers' College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayotzinapa_Rural_Teachers%27_College) disappeared after being detained by local police and allegedly handed over to a criminal group, became a flashpoint in Peña Nieto's administration. The government's investigation, which international forensic experts later discredited, provoked national and international condemnation and stressed the entanglement between authorities and organized crime.[^cite_note-126]&#x20;

In 2015, cartel leader [Joaquín "El Chapo" Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_%22El_Chapo%22_Guzm%C3%A1n) escaped from Mexico's maximum-security [Altiplano prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altiplano_prison) through a mile-long tunnel equipped with lighting, ventilation, and a rail-mounted motorcycle. After six months on the run, he [was recaptured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_recapture_of_El_Chapo) in [Los Mochis](14675780.md), [Sinaloa](222251.md), following a military raid. Guzmán was extradited to the United States in 2017, where he [was convicted on multiple charges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Guzm%C3%A1n) and sentenced to life imprisonment.[^cite_note-9kk9p-127][^cite_note-2U13n-128]&#x20;

A centerpiece of Peña Nieto's strategy consisted of making the Mexican [Interior Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_the_Interior_\(Mexico\)) solely responsible for public security and the creation of a national military-level police force called the [National Gendarmederie](9964117.md#Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto's_administration). In 2017, the [Law of Internal Security](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ley_de_Seguridad_Interior_\(M%C3%A9xico\)), which sought to formalize the military's presence in civilian law enforcement, was passed by the legislature but faced significant criticism for undermining civil liberties and was ultimately derogated by the Supreme Court a year later.[^cite_note-OgPEl-129][^cite_note-KJa24-130][^cite_note-zRt3e-131][^cite_note-qOEzP-132]&#x20;

### Andrés Manuel López Obrador

Main article: [Presidency of Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador)



President [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador)

[Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador), of the left-wing [Morena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morena_\(political_party\)) party, took office in 2018. One of his campaign promises was to grant amnesty to Mexicans coerced into drug production and trafficking.[^cite_note-Amnesty-133] His administration stressed that this would not apply to cartel members, but to poor farmers, coerced laborers, and young people jailed for drug possession.[^cite_note-MiLVs-134] López Obrador argued that previous strategies ignored social inequalities and left communities vulnerable. His strategy was described with the phrase "*[Abrazos, no balazos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrazos,_no_balazos)*" ('hugs, not bullets'), emphasizing [welfare spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_spending), scholarships, and youth employment programs, with security policy directed by [Rosa Icela Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Icela_Rodr%C3%ADguez) ([Secretariat of Security and Civilian Protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Security_and_Civilian_Protection)), [Alejandro Gertz Manero](44741930.md) (Attorney General), and [Luis Cresencio Sandoval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Cresencio_Sandoval) (National Defense).&#x20;

In January 2019, López Obrador declared "the end of the Mexican war on drugs",[^cite_note-5ZK16-135] stating that his administration would focus on reducing spending[^cite_note-El0fl-136] and direct its military and police efforts primarily on stopping oil theft rings—locally called *[huachicoleros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huachicolero)*[^cite_note-BxHvc-137][^cite_note-eermr-138][^cite_note-j5hzQ-139]—that targeted the Mexican state-owned company [Pemex](286846.md).[^cite_note-oMD5Z-140] In May 2019, the [Mexican National Guard](61282251.md) was created, merging units of the [Federal Police](9964117.md), military police, [Navy](1225277.md), the Chief of Staff's Guard, and other security agencies. While initially envisioned as a civilian-led force, it was eventually placed under the control of the [Secretariat of National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_National_Defense) (SEDENA), which triggered criticism for continuing military deployment despite López Obrador's pledge to withdraw the Army from the streets.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-xn3Ie-142][^cite_note-TKhrX-143]&#x20;

Drug-related violence remained at previous levels during the first months of López Obrador's presidency.[^cite_note-Q1uYc-144][^cite_note-BBC_Najar_Jan_2019-145] In October 2019, [a National Guard operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Culiac%C3%A1n) in [Culiacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culiac%C3%A1n), [Sinaloa](222251.md) to capture [Ovidio Guzmán López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovidio_Guzm%C3%A1n_L%C3%B3pez), responding to a U.S. extradition request, failed after cartel gunmen took hostages and forced his release.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] Guzmán was released after approximately 700 cartel enforcers took multiple hostages, including the housing unit where military families live in Culiacán.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] López Obrador defended the decision to release Guzmán López, arguing it prevented further loss of life,[^cite_note-151] and insisted that he wanted to avoid more massacres,[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] and that even though they underestimated the cartel's forces and ability to respond,[^cite_note-154] the criminal process against Ovidio is still ongoing.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-\:0-111] Guzmán López was captured in [a 2023 operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mongoose_Azteca) and extradited to the U.S.&#x20;

The strategy of avoiding armed confrontations while drug organizations have continued violent altercations has been controversial.[^cite_note-5ZK16-135][^cite_note-BBC_Najar_Jan_2019-145][^cite_note-eUUA2-156][^cite_note-HVwHc-157] The [Jalisco New Generation Cartel](35006872.md) (CJNG) expanded aggressively during López Obrador's presidency, and its territorial disputes were linked to significant increases in homicide rates in [Colima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colima), [Zacatecas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zacatecas), [Guanajuato](412827.md), and [Jalisco](16020434.md).[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] In July 2022, authorities captured [Rafael Caro-Quintero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Caro-Quintero), a former leader of the Guadalajara Cartel.[^cite_note-161]&#x20;

Despite his campaign promises, military deployments and expenditures have risen, with troop levels 76% higher and defense spending up 87% between 2012 and 2022.[^cite_note-\:4-162] While deployments expanded, available data indicate a more restrained operational role, with fewer confrontations and seizures, alongside new duties such as infrastructure projects and vaccine distribution.[^cite_note-\:4-162][^cite_note-163] Under U.S. pressure, the National Guard has been increasingly deployed to stem migrant flows from Central America.[^cite_note-164]&#x20;

### Claudia Sheinbaum

Main article: [Presidency of Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Claudia_Sheinbaum)

[Claudia Sheinbaum](57813311.md) has pledged continuity with López Obrador's security strategy. She appointed [Omar García Harfuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Garc%C3%ADa_Harfuch) to the [Secretariat of Security and Civilian Protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Security_and_Civilian_Protection) (SSPC). Under her administration, the SSPC's powers were expanded through legislation that facilitated closer collaboration with the [Attorney General's Office](2586359.md), and greater intelligence gathering and sharing across government agencies.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168]&#x20;

Her government also led targeted operations against [cartel infighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infighting_in_the_Sinaloa_Cartel), and a greater use of surveillance technology. [Nemesio Oseguera Cervantes](53322465.md), the leader of the Jalisco New Generation Cartel, was killed by Mexican Army during the [2026 Jalisco operation](82483857.md) on February 22, triggering widespread retaliatory violence and travel disruptions as the [2026 Mexico cartel unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Mexico_cartel_unrest) as due to the absence of leadership and ability of hardliners to push the state to a brink.[^cite_note-169]&#x20;

## Mexican cartels

See also: [List of Mexican cartels](82533550.md)



Map of [Mexican cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_cartel) headquarters

### Origins

Main article: [Crime in Mexico](5182370.md)

The birth of most Mexican drug cartels is traced to former Mexican Judicial Federal Police agent [Miguel Ángel Félix Gallardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_F%C3%A9lix_Gallardo) ([Spanish](26825.md): *El Padrino*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'The Godfather'), who founded the [Guadalajara Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara_Cartel) in 1980 and controlled most of the illegal drug trade in Mexico and the trafficking corridors across the Mexico–U.S. border along with [Juan García Ábrego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Garc%C3%ADa_%C3%81brego) throughout the 1980s.[^cite_note-Time-170] He started by smuggling marijuana and [opium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium) into the U.S., and was the first Mexican drug chief to link up with Colombia's cocaine [cartels](197015.md) in the 1980s. Through his connections, Félix Gallardo became the person at the forefront of the [Medellín Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medell%C3%ADn_Cartel), which was run by [Pablo Escobar](161570.md).[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171] This was accomplished because Félix Gallardo had already established a marijuana trafficking infrastructure that stood ready to serve the Colombia-based cocaine traffickers.&#x20;

There were no other cartels at that time in Mexico.[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171]<sup>: 41 </sup> He oversaw operations with his cronies and the politicians who sold him protection.[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171] The Guadalajara Cartel suffered a major blow in 1985 when the group's co-founder [Rafael Caro Quintero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Caro_Quintero) was captured, and later convicted, for the murder of DEA agent [Enrique "Kiki" Camarena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiki_Camarena).[^cite_note-J7rbM-172][^cite_note-Organized-173] Félix Gallardo then kept a low profile and in 1987 he moved with his family to [Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara). According to [Peter Dale Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Dale_Scott), the Guadalajara Cartel prospered largely because it enjoyed the protection of the [Dirección Federal de Seguridad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direcci%C3%B3n_Federal_de_Seguridad) (DFS), under its chief [Miguel Nazar Haro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Nazar_Haro).[^cite_note-autogenerated7-174]&#x20;

Félix Gallardo was arrested on April 8, 1989.[^cite_note-NYTimes-175] He then divested the trade he controlled, as it would be more efficient and less likely to be brought down in one law enforcement swoop.[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171]<sup>: 47 </sup> He sent his lawyer to convene the nation's top drug traffickers at a house in [Acapulco](1446.md) where he designated *plazas* or territories.[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171][^cite_note-up4KF-176]&#x20;

The Tijuana route would go to his nephews, the [Arellano Felix brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuana_Cartel). The [Ciudad Juárez route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ju%C3%A1rez_Cartel) would go to the [Carrillo Fuentes family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amado_Carrillo_Fuentes). [Miguel Caro Quintero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Caro_Quintero) would run the [Sonora corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonora_Cartel). Meanwhile, [Joaquín Guzmán Loera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_Guzm%C3%A1n_Loera) and [Ismael Zambada García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismael_Zambada_Garc%C3%ADa) would take over Pacific coast operations, becoming the [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md). Guzmán and Zambada brought veteran [Héctor Luis Palma Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9ctor_Luis_Palma_Salazar) back into the fold. The control of the Matamoros, Tamaulipas corridor—then becoming the [Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Cartel)—would be left undisturbed to its founder, Juan García Ábrego, who was not a party to the 1989 pact.[^cite_note-PkItr-177]&#x20;

Félix Gallardo still planned to oversee national operations, as he maintained important connections, but he would no longer control all details of the business.[^cite_note-Last_Narco-171] When he was transferred to a high-security prison in 1993, he lost any remaining control over the other cartel leaders.[^cite_note-ybq95-178]&#x20;

### Major cartels in the war

Main article: [Drug cartel § Mexico](197015.md#Mexico)

#### Sinaloa Cartel

Main article: [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md)



Drug trafficking tunnel under the [U.S.-Mexico border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border) from [Tijuana](101739.md) to [Otay Mesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otay_Mesa) used by the Sinaloa Cartel

The Sinaloa Cartel began to contest the Gulf Cartel's domination of the southwest Texas corridor following the arrest of Gulf Cartel leader [Osiel Cárdenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiel_C%C3%A1rdenas) in March 2003. In 2006, it formalized "the Federation," an alliance of criminal groups in Sinaloa under [Joaquín "El Chapo" Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_%22El_Chapo%22_Guzm%C3%A1n), who became Mexico's most-wanted trafficker, with an estimated net worth of US\$1 billion.[^cite_note-Xb4an-179]&#x20;



Guzmán's lieutenant [Alfredo Beltrán Leyva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva) ("El Mochomo") was arrested in 2008.

Under Guzmán, the Sinaloa Cartel fought the Juárez Cartel in a prolonged battle for control over drug trafficking routes in and around Ciudad Juárez. The battle resulted in defeat for the Juárez Cartel, resulting in the deaths of between 5,000 and 12,000 people.[^cite_note-autogenerated3-180] The Sinaloa Cartel used several gangs (e.g. [Los Mexicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Mexicles), the [Artistas Asesinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artistas_Asesinos) and [Gente Nueva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gente_Nueva)) to attack the Juárez Cartel. The Juárez Cartel similarly used gangs such as [La Línea](26470938.md) and the [Barrio Azteca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrio_Azteca) to fight the Sinaloa Cartel.[^cite_note-autogenerated3-180][^cite_note-burnett1-181][^cite_note-burnett2-182][^cite_note-M2Mo4-183] In February 2010, the Federation formed new alliances against Los Zetas and the [Beltrán-Leyva Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beltr%C3%A1n-Leyva_Cartel).[^cite_note-MexiData-184]&#x20;

Guzmán was arrested and escaped in 2015,[^cite_note-wd6y1-185][^cite_note-xYLdr-186] re-arrested in 2016,[^cite_note-dFBbJ-187] and extradited to the U.S. in 2017.[^cite_note-188] Guzmán claimed that he had paid former presidents [Enrique Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto) and [Felipe Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n) bribes, which both denied.[^cite_note-189] In 2019, he was convicted of drug trafficking and sentenced to life imprisonment, after which [Ismael "El Mayo" Zambada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismael_%22El_Mayo%22_Zambada) emerged as the cartel's senior figure. Zambada was arrested in 2024 and extradited to the U.S. in 2025.[^cite_note-fearedcapo-190][^cite_note-191]&#x20;

In January 2023, the arrest of Guzmán's son [Ovidio Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovidio_Guzm%C3%A1n_L%C3%B3pez) sparked [a wave of violence in the state of Sinaloa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Sinaloa_unrest), with the military deploying aircraft and heavy forces in response.[^cite_note-192] Following the arrests of Guzmán and Zambada, the Sinaloa Cartel has [experienced infighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infighting_in_the_Sinaloa_Cartel), with rival factions aligning under their respective names.&#x20;

In February 2026, the [U.S. State Department](31975.md) says it will pay up to \$10 million for information leading to the arrests or convictions of two brothers identified as leaders of Mexico's [Sinaloa cartel](16881931.md) in the state of [Baja California](199793.md).[^cite_note-193]&#x20;

#### Beltrán-Leyva Cartel

Main article: [Beltrán-Leyva Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beltr%C3%A1n-Leyva_Cartel)

The Beltrán-Leyva Cartel was founded by the four Beltrán Leyva brothers: [Marcos Arturo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Arturo_Beltr%C3%A1n-Leyva), [Carlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva), [Alfredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva) and [Héctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9ctor_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva).[^cite_note-yH7ac-194][^cite_note-cyCaf-195][^cite_note-zT894-196][^cite_note-Q1wNi-197] In 2004 and 2005, cartel leader Arturo Beltrán Leyva led groups of enforcers to compete for trafficking routes in northeastern Mexico against the Sinaloa Cartel. The Beltrán-Leyva Cartel infiltrated Mexico's political,[^cite_note-1b8oK-198] judicial,[^cite_note-zaLs9-199] and police institutions, including the [Interpol](14338129.md) in Mexico,[^cite_note-Zyl4P-200] to feed classified information about anti-drug operations against its rivals.[^cite_note-Hch0L-201][^cite_note-Jc9sd-202]&#x20;

Following the 2009 killing of [Arturo Beltrán Leyva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva), the cartel entered into an internal power struggle between Arturo's brother, Héctor Beltrán Leyva, and his top enforcer [Edgar Valdez Villarreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Valdez_Villarreal).[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10] Meanwhile, the cartel continued to dissolve with factions such as the [South Pacific Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Pacific_Cartel), La Mano Con Ojos, [Independent Cartel of Acapulco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Cartel_of_Acapulco), and [La Barredora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Barredora) forming and the latter two cartels starting yet another intra-Beltrán Leyva Cartel conflict.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10] The Mexican Federal Police considers the cartel to have been disbanded,[^cite_note-ftogB-203][^cite_note-XdSPc-204] and their last leader, Héctor Beltrán Leyva, was captured in October 2014.[^cite_note-DOS-205]&#x20;

#### Juárez Cartel

Main article: [Juárez Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ju%C3%A1rez_Cartel)

The Juárez Cartel controls one of the primary transportation routes for billions of dollars' worth of illegal drug shipments annually entering the United States from Mexico.[^cite_note-3Pv29-206] Since 2007, the Juárez Cartel has been locked in a vicious battle with its former partner, the Sinaloa Cartel, for control of Ciudad Juárez. [La Línea](26470938.md) is a group of Mexican drug traffickers and corrupt Juárez and Chihuahua state police officers who work as the armed wing of the Juárez Cartel.[^cite_note-LxLfK-207] [Vicente Carrillo Fuentes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Carrillo_Fuentes) headed the Juárez Cartel until his arrest in 2014.&#x20;

Since 2011, the Juárez Cartel has continued to weaken.[^cite_note-e3usl-208][^cite_note-Plbpk-209] It is present in the three main points of entry into [El Paso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Paso), [Texas](29810.md). Its weakness and inability to effectively fight against Sinaloa's advances in Juárez contributed to the lower death toll in Juárez in 2011.[^cite_note-stratfor.com-210]&#x20;

#### Tijuana Cartel

Main article: [Tijuana Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuana_Cartel)



[Francisco Javier Arellano Félix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Javier_Arellano_F%C3%A9lix), the Tijuana Cartel leader, was captured by the DEA.

The Tijuana Cartel, also known as the Arellano Félix Organization, was once among Mexico's most powerful.[^cite_note-PYoKZ-211] It is based in Tijuana, one of the most strategically important border towns in Mexico,[^cite_note-KmnYH-212] and continues to export drugs even after being weakened by an internal war in 2009. Due to infighting, arrests, and the deaths of some of its top members, the Tijuana Cartel is a fraction of what it was in the 1990s and early 2000s. After the arrest or assassination of various members of the Arellano Félix family, the cartel is currently allegedly headed by Edwin Huerta Nuño, alias "El Flako".&#x20;

#### Gulf Cartel

Main article: [Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Cartel)



Mexican Army raids a Gulf Cartel's house in [Matamoros, Tamaulipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matamoros,_Tamaulipas), in 2012

Based in [Matamoros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matamoros,_Tamaulipas), [Tamaulipas](222254.md), the Gulf Cartel (CDG) (*Spanish: Cártel del Golfo*) has long been one of Mexico's dominant criminal organizations. Its former arrangement with their armed wing, [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas), collapsed in 2010, and both groups engaged in widespread violence across several border cities of Tamaulipas state,[^cite_note-MexiData-184][^cite_note-Zetas-Golfo-213] turning several border towns into "ghost towns".[^cite_note-Of9BK-214]&#x20;

The CDG held off several Los Zetas incursions into its territory. However, by 2011, internal divisions led to intra-cartel battles in Matamoros and [Reynosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reynosa). [Infighting in the Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infighting_in_the_Gulf_Cartel) resulted in several arrests and deaths in Mexico and in the United States. The CDG has since broken apart, and it appears that one of its factions, known as [Los Metros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Metros), has overpowered its rival, [Los Rojos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Rojos), and is now asserting its control over CDG operations.[^cite_note-stratfor.com-210] The Mexican federal government captured several leaders of the CDG during [Joint Operation Nuevo León-Tamaulipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Operation_Nuevo_Le%C3%B3n-Tamaulipas). [Osiel Cárdenas Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiel_C%C3%A1rdenas_Guill%C3%A9n), his brothers [Antonio Cárdenas Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_C%C3%A1rdenas_Guill%C3%A9n), [Mario Cárdenas Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_C%C3%A1rdenas_Guill%C3%A9n), and [Jorge Eduardo Costilla Sánchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Eduardo_Costilla_S%C3%A1nchez) were captured and incarcerated during Felipe Calderón's administration.&#x20;

#### Los Zetas

Main article: [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas)



Leadership chart of the [Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Cartel) and [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas) issued by the U.S. Department of the Treasury, March 2010

Los Zetas originated in 1999, when Gulf Cartel leader [Osiel Cárdenas Guillén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiel_C%C3%A1rdenas_Guill%C3%A9n) recruited 37 former elite soldiers from Mexico's special forces to serve as his armed wing. Known as Los Zetas, they operated as the cartel's private army and played a central role in its dominance of the drug trade in the early 2000s.[^cite_note-MexiData-184]&#x20;

After Cárdenas Guillén's 2007 arrest and extradition, Los Zetas broke away under [Heriberto Lazcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heriberto_Lazcano), building independent networks in drug, arms, and human trafficking. By 2008, they had allied with the Beltrán Leyva brothers, turning against their former partners, the Gulf Cartel.[^cite_note-u9p1W-215] In 2010, the split became open war, with Los Zetas and the Gulf Cartel fighting for control of routes in northeast Mexico, which resulted in thousands of deaths.[^cite_note-MexiData-184][^cite_note-s3ZGL-216] In July 2013, the Mexican Navy arrested leader [Miguel Treviño Morales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Trevi%C3%B1o_Morales).[^cite_note-Jf8ZT-217]&#x20;

Los Zetas are notorious for targeting civilians, including the mass murder of 72 migrants in the [San Fernando massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_San_Fernando_massacre).[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10] Their activities extended beyond narcotics and have also been connected to [human trafficking](19333662.md), oil theft from pipelines, extortion, and [digital piracy](18948365.md). Their criminal network is said to reach far from Mexico, including into Central America, the U.S., and Europe.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10]&#x20;

In recent times, Los Zetas have undergone [fragmentation and infighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infighting_in_Los_Zetas) and seen a decline in their influence.[^cite_note-218] By the late 2010s, the group had fragmented into rival factions such as [Sangre Nueva Zeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangre_Nueva_Zeta) and [Zetas Vieja Escuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zetas_Vieja_Escuela), some of which allied with the Gulf Cartel against the [Cártel del Noreste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A1rtel_del_Noreste). Remnants of Los Zetas have also operated under the Cártel del Noreste name.[^cite_note-borderlandbeat-219]&#x20;

#### La Familia Michoacana Cartel

Main article: [La Familia Michoacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Familia_Michoacana)



Map of Mexican drug cartels presence in Mexico based on a May 2010 [Stratfor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratfor) report

[^cite_note-zeBYc-220]

[^cite_note-hMQXZ-221]

&#x20; [Tijuana Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuana_Cartel)

&#x20; [Beltrán-Leyva Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beltr%C3%A1n-Leyva_Cartel)

&#x20; [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md)

&#x20; [Juárez Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ju%C3%A1rez_Cartel)

&#x20; [La Familia Michoacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Familia_Michoacana)

&#x20; [Gulf Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Cartel)

&#x20; [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas)

&#x20; Disputed territories

La Familia Michoacana was a drug cartel based in [Michoacán](195805.md) between at least 2006 and 2011. It was formerly allied with the Gulf Cartel and Los Zetas, but split off to become an independent organization.[^cite_note-L2X1k-222] La Familia Michoacana was the first cartel targeted by President Calderón's security strategy, beginning with [Operation Michoacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Michoac%C3%A1n) in December 2006. Between 2009 and 2010, joint U.S.–Mexican operations led to the arrest of hundreds of La Familia members and the reported death of founder [Nazario Moreno González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazario_Moreno_Gonz%C3%A1lez) ("El Más Loco").[^cite_note-zUI5w-223] The group soon split between the [Knights Templar Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Templar_Cartel) and a faction led by [José de Jesús Méndez Vargas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_Jes%C3%BAs_M%C3%A9ndez_Vargas) ("El Chango"), who was arrested in 2011, after which authorities declared La Familia dismantled, leaving the Knights Templar as its *de facto* successor.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10][^cite_note-Aug_2011-224][^cite_note-Raid-225][^cite_note-xSF82-226]&#x20;

In February 2010, La Familia allied with the Gulf Cartel against Los Zetas and the Beltrán-Leyva Cartel.[^cite_note-MexiData-184] [La Nueva Familia Michoacana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Nueva_Familia_Michoacana_Organization) emerged from splinters of La Familia in the early 2010s, rebranding while continuing operations in Michoacán and Guerrero, and clashing with the Jalisco New Generation Cartel.&#x20;

#### Knights Templar

Main article: [Knights Templar Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Templar_Cartel)

The Knights Templar Cartel (Spanish: *Caballeros Templarios*) was created in Michoacán in 2011 after the presumed death of the leader of La Familia Michoacana, Nazario Moreno González.[^cite_note-Tunics-227] The cartel was headed by [Enrique Plancarte Solís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Plancarte_Sol%C3%ADs) and [Servando Gómez Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Servando_G%C3%B3mez_Mart%C3%ADnez) ("La Tuta"), who formed the Knights Templar due to differences with José de Jesús Méndez Vargas, who had assumed leadership of La Familia Michoacana.[^cite_note-33X48-228] The cartel was reported to promote a religious doctrine as part of its identity.[^cite_note-229]&#x20;

Sizable battles flared up in 2011 between the Knights Templar and La Familia.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10] The organization grew from a splinter group to a dominant force in Michoacán, and following the arrest of Méndez Vargas, the cartel appeared to have taken over the bulk of La Familia's operations.[^cite_note-ReferenceC-10] In 2011 the Knights Templar appeared to have aligned with the Sinaloa Federation in an effort to root out the remnants of La Familia and to prevent Los Zetas from gaining foothold in the Michoacán region.[^cite_note-XGrf4-230][^cite_note-THMtt-231] In 2014 Plancarte Solís was killed by the Mexican Navy, and Gómez Martínez was arrested in 2015. The cartel is believed to have disbanded in 2017.&#x20;

#### CJNG

Main article: [Jalisco New Generation Cartel](35006872.md)



Area of influence map of the [Jalisco New Generation Cartel](35006872.md) in the United States as of 2017

The **Jalisco New Generation Cartel** (CJNG) (Spanish: *Cártel de Jalisco Nueva Generación*)[^cite_note-auto-232][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] is a Mexican criminal group based in [Jalisco](16020434.md) and was headed by [Nemesio Oseguera Cervantes](53322465.md) ("El Mencho"), who was Mexico's most-wanted cartel leader until his death in 2026.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] The CJNG started as one of the splits of [Milenio Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milenio_Cartel), beside [La Resistencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Resistencia_\(gang\)). The CJNG defeated La Resistencia and took control of Millenio Cartel's smuggling networks. The cartel expanded its operation network from coast to coast in only six months, making it one of the criminal groups with the greatest operating capacity in Mexico as of 2012.[^cite_note-238]&#x20;

Through online messaging, the Jalisco New Generation Cartel has tried to seek social approval and tacit consent from the Mexican government to confront Los Zetas by posing as a "righteous" and "nationalist" group.[^cite_note-justice-239][^cite_note-240] By 2018 the CJNG was claimed to be the most powerful cartel in Mexico,[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] though Insight Crime has said the Sinaloa Cartel is still the most powerful cartel and the CJNG its closest rival.[^cite_note-fearedcapo-190][^cite_note-244] In 2019, the group was weakened by infighting, arrests of senior operatives, and a war with the Sinaloa Cartel and its allies.[^cite_note-cjng2019-245]&#x20;

### Cartel affiliates and street gangs

Smaller drug cartels and localized street gangs operate across Mexico. These gangs can control retail drug markets, extortion rackets, and serve as enforcers of cartels. In Ciudad Juárez, for example, [La Línea](26470938.md), historically allied with the Juárez Cartel, and [Los Mexicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Mexicles), aligned with the Sinaloa Cartel, have been responsible for major spikes of violence, and both groups maintain ties to U.S.-based gangs.[^cite_note-246] In Jalisco and Michoacán, the Jalisco New Generation Cartel has recently allied with gangs such as [Los Viagras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Viagras) (a former *autodefensa* group), to fight for territory and run oil theft operations, despite the two groups having been bitter rivals throughout the 2010s.[^cite_note-247]&#x20;

While cartels are sometimes portrayed as centralized, hierarchical organizations, they often function more as loose networks of cells and affiliates that can shift loyalties or rebrand over time. This structure can make them resilient, but it also fuels infighting and fragmentation, contributing to persistent and unpredictable violence even when leaders are captured.[^cite_note-248] Even long-standing cartels have experienced prolonged, violent internal disputes, such as the [Sinaloa Cartel infighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infighting_in_the_Sinaloa_Cartel) in the 2020s.[^cite_note-249]&#x20;

### Paramilitaries and other sources of income

Beyond drug trafficking, Mexican cartels derive revenue from activities including [extortion](162449.md), [kidnapping](169376.md), [oil theft from pipelines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huachicol), [human smuggling](68714.md), [illegal mining](49640132.md) and [logging](465780.md), [arms trafficking](42210.md), [sex trafficking](2101268.md), and [protection rackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protection_rackets) in territories under their control. The fragmentation of larger cartels into regional groups has extended their operations beyond traditional strongholds in [Michoacán](195805.md), [Guerrero](195816.md), and [Northern Mexico](48157875.md), with organized crime now present in nearly every Mexican state.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]&#x20;

Paramilitary groups work alongside cartels to enforce these activities. It has been suggested that the rise in paramilitary groups coincides with a loss of security within the government. These paramilitary groups came about in a number of ways. First, waves of elite armed forces and government security experts have left the government to join the side of the cartels, responding to large bribes. Some of the elite armed forces members who join paramilitaries are trained in the [Western Hemisphere Institute for Security Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Hemisphere_Institute_for_Security_Cooperation) (WHINSEC, formerly known as the School of the Americas). One theory suggests that paramilitaries have emerged from the deregulation of the Mexican army, which private security firms have gradually replaced.[^cite_note-RZBx7-252]&#x20;

### Cartel propaganda and messaging

Main article: [Propaganda in the Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_in_the_Mexican_drug_war)

Drug cartels in Mexico are heavily involved in [public relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_relations) and [information warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_warfare), employing tools such as food handouts, social media accounts, press release-style videos, [narco corridos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narco_corridos), and group chats through private messaging platforms primarily [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp) and [Telegram](41501305.md). Cartel propaganda seeks to influence public opinion, threaten or discredit rivals, and coordinate between organizations.[^cite_note-253] Physical "narco messages", ranging from printed banners to handwritten notes, are often displayed in public spaces or left at crime scenes. Some groups, notably the [Jalisco New Generation Cartel](35006872.md) (CJNG), maintain dedicated propaganda arms producing coordinated messages with logos, slogans, and professional formatting.[^cite_note-254] Propaganda is also directed at cartel members themselves, with organizations such as La Familia Michoacana and the Knights Templar promoting religious, mythologizing narratives to reinforce loyalty. In 2011, President [Felipe Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n) met with major media outlets, urging them to reduce sensationalist coverage and limit the dissemination of cartel messaging.[^cite_note-255] Many cartels tacitly control local information environments by threatening journalists, bloggers, and others who speak out against them.&#x20;

In recent years, cartel messaging has moved into [social media](5897742.md) [short-form videos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-form_content) and imagery that glamorize cartel life, weapons, and loyalty.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257] Cartel slang is increasingly echoed in popular culture, blurring between criminal identity signifiers and everyday [Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Spanish). Some journalists and researchers have used the term *[narcoculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcoculture_in_Mexico)* to describe this blend of subcultural references, while others have criticized the label as sensationalistic, noting that cartel recruitment also relies heavily on coercion, patronage networks, and legal businesses. The concept nonetheless points to how cultural symbols and narratives can help cartels normalize their presence and project power.[^cite_note-NuñezGonzalez-258][^cite_note-BecerraRomero-259]&#x20;

## Socioeconomic and structural factors

### Government corruption

See also: [Corruption in Mexico](39500474.md)



Former [Secretary of Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretariat_of_Public_Security) [Genaro García Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genaro_Garc%C3%ADa_Luna) was found guilty on multiple charges related to corruption and drug trafficking. He is the highest-ranking Mexican official ever to be convicted in the United States.

[^cite_note-GarciaLuna-260]

Mexican cartels advance their operations, in part, by corrupting or intimidating law enforcement officials.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-Aponte-107] Mexican municipal, state, and federal government officials, along with the police forces, often work together with the cartels [in an organized network of corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_corruption_in_Mexico).[^cite_note-Vulliamy_2010-261] A *Pax Mafioso* is a specific example of corruption that guarantees a politician votes and a following in exchange for not impeding a particular cartel.[^cite_note-Vulliamy_2010-261]&#x20;

The [International Narcotics Control Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Narcotics_Control_Board) (INCB) reports that although the central government of Mexico has made concerted efforts to reduce corruption in recent years, it remains a serious problem.[^cite_note-Zyl4P-200][^cite_note-0cFeH-262] Agents of the now-defunct [Federal Investigations Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Investigations_Agency) (AFI) were believed to work as enforcers for various cartels. The [Attorney General](2586359.md) (PGR) reported in December 2005 that nearly 1,500 of AFI's 7,000 agents were under investigation for suspected criminal activity and 457 were facing charges.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60]&#x20;



[El Azul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Esparragoza_Moreno) was a Sinaloa Cartel leader. He was a former [Dirección Federal de Seguridad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direcci%C3%B3n_Federal_de_Seguridad) (DFS) agent.

In recent years, the federal government conducted purges and prosecutions of police forces in Nuevo Laredo, Michoacán, Baja California, and Mexico City.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60] The anti-cartel operations begun by President Calderón in December 2006 include ballistic checks of police weapons in places where there is concern that police are also working for the cartels. In June 2007, President Calderón purged 284 federal police commanders from all 31 states and the Federal District.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60]&#x20;

Under the 'Cleanup Operation' performed in 2008, several agents and high-ranking officials have been arrested and charged with selling information or protection to drug cartels;[^cite_note-4Rv5u-263][^cite_note-o1DDW-264] some high-profile arrests were: Victor Gerardo Garay Cadena,[^cite_note-KGh6b-265] (chief of the Federal Police), [Noé Ramírez Mandujano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No%C3%A9_Ram%C3%ADrez_Mandujano) (ex-chief of the [Organized Crime Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEIDO) (SEIDO)), [José Luis Santiago Vasconcelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Luis_Santiago_Vasconcelos) (ex-chief of SEIDO), and Ricardo Gutiérrez Vargas who is the ex-director of Mexico's Interpol office. In January 2009, Rodolfo de la Guardia García, ex-director of Mexico's Interpol office, was arrested.[^cite_note-L7RUV-266] [Julio César Godoy Toscano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julio_C%C3%A9sar_Godoy_Toscano), who was elected in July 2009 to the lower house of Congress, was charged with being a top-ranking member of La Familia Michoacana.[^cite_note-jV2yC-267] He is now a fugitive.&#x20;

In May 2010, an [NPR](29697232.md) report collected allegations from dozens of sources, including U.S. and Mexican media, Mexican police officials, politicians, academics, and others, that Sinaloa Cartel had infiltrated and corrupted the Mexican federal government and the Mexican military by bribery and other means.[^cite_note-kJTei-268] The report also stated that the Sinaloa Cartel was colluding with the government to destroy other cartels and protect itself and its leader, "El Chapo" Guzmán. Mexican officials have denied any corruption in the government's treatment of cartels.[^cite_note-burnett1-181][^cite_note-burnett2-182]&#x20;

### Law enforcement

Main article: [Law enforcement in Mexico](19426382.md)



The Mexican [Federal Police](9964117.md) was formed in 1999 and disbanded in 2019.

Mexico has thousands of municipal police forces, with uneven training, resources, and oversight. Smaller local forces can be easily co-opted by cartels, while state and federal bodies often duplicate or conflict with them. Police departments often depend on mayors or governors for salaries and resources, while governments periodically "purge" police departments by mass firings, then rehiring with little institutional continuity.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-DanielSabet-269] Federal law enforcement has been reorganized at least five times since 1982, and at least four [special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_forces) units have been created.[^cite_note-sPnCp-71]&#x20;

[Internal affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_affairs_\(law_enforcement\)) and civilian oversight of police and military are limited, and [whistleblowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whistleblowers) risk retaliation. Mexico's public ministry has been criticized for poor case handling, with little forensic work, reliance on forced confessions, and a lack of [chain of custody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chain_of_custody) protections, which allow many suspects to be released on technicalities.[^cite_note-impunity1-270][^cite_note-impunity2-271] Cartels have been reported as difficult to prosecute "because members of the cartels have infiltrated and corrupted the law enforcement organizations that are supposed to prosecute them, such as the Office of the [Attorney General](2586359.md)."[^cite_note-Zamora_Jimenez2003-272] [Impunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impunity) rates for violent crime in Mexico are estimated at between 90 and 95%.[^cite_note-impunity1-270][^cite_note-impunity2-271]&#x20;

### Weaponry and equipment

Main article: [Smuggling of firearms into Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_of_firearms_into_Mexico)

See also: [Gun politics in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_Mexico)



[AK-47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47) (locally called *cuerno de chivo*, Spanish for "goat horn", for its curved magazine)



[M4 carbine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_carbine) with [grenade launcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenade_launcher) (locally called *[chanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanate)*, Mexican Spanish for "[great-tailed grackle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great-tailed_grackle)")



[Colt AR-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colt_AR-15) A3 tactical carbine

Mexicans have a constitutional [right to own firearms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_own_firearms),[^cite_note-Arms_Trafficking-273] but legal purchase is highly restricted.[^cite_note-sZ6qR-274] The most common weapons used by the cartels are the [AR-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AR-15), [M16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M16_rifle), [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_carbine), [AK-47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47), [AKM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKM) and [Type 56](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_56_assault_rifle) [assault rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assault_rifles), which are not available for sale in civilian markets. Grenade launchers are known to have been used against Mexican security forces, while [H\&K G36s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heckler_%26_Koch_G36) and M4 carbines with [M203 grenade launchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M203_grenade_launcher) have been confiscated. Some cartels, such as the [Beltrán Leyva Cartel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beltr%C3%A1n_Leyva_Cartel), use counterfeit M16s made with aftermarket parts.[^cite_note-275] Cartels also use [body armor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballistic_vest), [Kevlar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevlar) helmets, [improvised explosive devices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvised_explosive_devices), [narco-submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narco-submarine) and [unmanned aerial vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unmanned_aerial_vehicles).[^cite_note-p94mL-276][^cite_note-snVjc-277][^cite_note-2bCHR-278][^cite_note-5vMW2-279][^cite_note-stratfor-280][^cite_note-rzjIE-281][^cite_note-pRuBA-282][^cite_note-XMIn4-283][^cite_note-GaZgK-284][^cite_note-efym9-285]&#x20;

Grenades and rocket launchers are often smuggled through the Guatemalan borders, as leftovers from past conflicts in Central America, notably the [Salvadoran Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Civil_War) and the [Nicaraguan Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution).[^cite_note-heavy_weapons-286] Some explosive weapons are also smuggled from the U.S. to Mexico[^cite_note-reuters.com-287] or stolen from the Mexican military.[^cite_note-auto2-288]&#x20;

The U.S. [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md) (DEA) reports that the Mexican drug cartels operating today along the border are far more sophisticated and dangerous than any other organized criminal group in U.S. law enforcement history.[^cite_note-rightside-289] [Project Gunrunner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Gunrunner) was a United States [Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Alcohol,_Tobacco,_Firearms_and_Explosives) (ATF) operation aimed at curbing firearms trafficking into Mexico.[^cite_note-Gunrunner-290] The project intended to stop the flow of firearms from the United States into Mexico and deny cartels weapons considered 'tools of the trade.'&#x20;

In 2011, [a gunwalking scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATF_gunwalking_scandal), later known as "Operation Fast and Furious," occurred when the ATF was accused of permitting and facilitating "[straw purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straw_purchase)" firearm sales to traffickers, and allowing the guns to "walk" and be transported to Mexico. Allegedly, the ATF allowed to complete the transactions to expose the supply chain and gather intelligence.[^cite_note-CBS_News-291][^cite_note-IYtWW-292] It has been established that this operation violated long-established ATF policies and practices and that it is not a recognized investigative technique.[^cite_note-5oyad-293] Several of the guns sold under the Project Gunrunner were recovered from crime scenes in Arizona,[^cite_note-ATF_Weapons-294] and at crime scenes throughout Mexico,[^cite_note-Fast_and_Furious_Map-295] resulting in considerable controversy.[^cite_note-CBS_News-291][^cite_note-4mM47-296][^cite_note-CBS_2008-297] One notable incident was the "[Black Swan operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_recapture_of_El_Chapo)" where Joaquín Guzmán Loera was finally captured. The ATF confirmed that one of the weapons the Mexican Navy seized from Guzmán's gunmen was one of the many weapons that were "lost" during the Project Gunrunner.[^cite_note-2n5ZK-298][^cite_note-ANwEW-299]&#x20;

Researchers and Mexican officials have argued that most weapons trafficked into Mexico originate from the United States.[^cite_note-AZTtV-300] The [U.S. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Homeland_Security) (DHS) officials have stated that the statistic is misleading: out of approximately 30,000 weapons seized in drug cases in Mexico in 2004–2008, 7,200 appeared to be of U.S. origin, approximately 4,000 were found in ATF manufacturer and importer records, and 87 percent of those—3,480—originated in the United States.[^cite_note-gao.gov-301][^cite_note-StratFor-302] The U.S. has continued to assist the Mexican government with technology, equipment, training and intelligence.[^cite_note-5D8rf-303][^cite_note-OIG_page_1-304] However, critics argue that [gun politics in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_the_United_States) have exacerbated the conflict by enabling the flow of weapons south of the border.&#x20;

### Supply chains

See also: [Illegal drug trade § Mexico](218449.md#Mexico)



Map of Mexican cartels' drug traffic routes in Mexico based on a 2012 Stratfor report



The states where most of the conflict took place in 2010, marked in red

According to the [National Drug Intelligence Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Drug_Intelligence_Center), Mexican cartels are the predominant smugglers and wholesale distributors of South American [cocaine](7701.md) and Mexico-produced [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis), [methamphetamine](11038318.md), and [heroin](14034.md). The [U.S. State Department](31975.md) estimates that 90 percent of cocaine entering the United States is produced in Colombia, followed by [Bolivia](3462.md) and [Peru](170691.md), and that the main transit route is through Mexico.[^cite_note-eMfeK-305][^cite_note-25fvm-306][^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-0ICtR-307] Mexican cartels control large swaths of territory, and most of the illegal drugs coming into the U.S., employing land routes, maritime shipments, [smuggling tunnels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smuggling_tunnel), and other concealment methods.[^cite_note-rightside-289][^cite_note-mh1q7-308] Cartels have waged violent turf battles over control of key smuggling corridors from Matamoros to Tijuana.&#x20;

Cartels operate [clandestine laboratories](610329.md) that process [precursor chemicals](36201623.md) into [synthetic drugs](70322626.md). These chemicals are primarily imported from Asia, especially China and India, and are used to manufacture methamphetamine and [fentanyl](141915.md) in large quantities.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-309][^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-iVeQv-310][^cite_note-stnFB-311] Since the 2010s, fentanyl has become one of the most profitable components of cartel operations due to its low production cost and high potency.[^cite_note-Felbab-Brown-312]&#x20;

Although Mexico accounts for only a small share of worldwide heroin production, it supplies a large share of the heroin distributed in the United States.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60] Since the 2000s, Mexican cartels have profited from marijuana cultivation in remote U.S. forests.[^cite_note-0Nern-313] A 2018 study found that the reduction in drugs from Colombia contributed to Mexican drug violence. The study estimated that "between 2006 and 2009, the decline in cocaine supply from Colombia could account for 10%–14% of the increase in violence in Mexico."[^cite_note-hmy2Q-314]&#x20;

The U.S. has imposed [sanctions](411315.md) against cartel members and their [front companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_companies). Multilateral cooperation in the [International Narcotics Control Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Narcotics_Control_Board) has promoted the regulation of chemical precursors used in the production of synthetic drugs.[^cite_note-315] Cartels have adapted by diversifying their markets and suppliers, expanding beyond the United States, and sourcing chemicals through multiple international channels.[^cite_note-Felbab-Brown-312]&#x20;

### Poverty

Main article: [Poverty in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Mexico)

One of the main factors driving the Mexican drug war is widespread poverty. From 2004 to 2008, the portion of the population who received less than half of the [median income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) rose from 17% to 21%, and the proportion of the population living in [extreme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_poverty) or moderate poverty rose from 35% to 46% (52 million persons) between 2006 and 2010.[^cite_note-kvZv6-316][^cite_note-oecd.org-317][^cite_note-ujtJw-318]&#x20;

Among the OECD countries, Mexico has the second-highest economic disparity between the extremely poor and the rich.[^cite_note-xoL0E-319] The bottom ten percent in the income hierarchy has 1.36% of the country's resources, whereas the upper ten percent has almost 36%. The OECD also notes that Mexico's budgeted [poverty alleviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_alleviation) and [social development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_change) expenses are only about a third of the OECD average.[^cite_note-oecd.org-317]&#x20;

In 2012, it was estimated that Mexican cartels employed over 450,000 people directly, and a further 3.2 million people's livelihoods depended on various parts of the drug trade.[^cite_note-RZBx7-252] In cities such as Ciudad Juárez, up to 60% of the economy depended on illegal sources of income.[^cite_note-IcOyA-320] As of 2023, an estimated 175,000 people are working for the cartels.[^cite_note-\:3-321] The head of the U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) reported that there are an estimated 45,000 members, associates, and brokers spread over more than 100 countries working under the Sinaloa cartel and the Jalisco New Generation cartel.[^cite_note-\:3-321]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Mexico](1179798.md)

A problem that goes hand in hand with poverty in Mexico is the level of [schooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schooling).[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] In the 1960s, when Mexican narcotic smugglers started to smuggle drugs on a major scale,[^cite_note-Vulliamy_2010-261] only 5.6% of the Mexican population had more than six years of schooling.[^cite_note-alba-324]&#x20;

More recently, researchers from the [World Economic Forum](172185.md) have noted that despite the Mexican economy ranking 31st out of 134 economies for investment in education (5.3% of its GDP), as of 2009, the nation's primary education system is ranked only 116th, thereby suggesting "that the problem is not how much but rather how resources are invested".[^cite_note-DmdRJ-325] The WEF further explained: "The powerful teachers union, the [SNTE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindicato_Nacional_de_Trabajadores_de_la_Educaci%C3%B3n), the largest labor union in Latin America, has been largely responsible for blocking reforms that would increase the quality of spending and help ensure equal access to education."&#x20;

Teachers in the Acapulco region were "extorted, kidnapped, and intimidated" by cartels, including death threats demanding money. They went on strike in 2011.[^cite_note-rTRMI-326]&#x20;

## Effects in Mexico

### Casualties

| Year | Killed |
| --- | --- |
| 2007 | 2,774 |
| 2008 | 5,679 |
| 2009 | 8,281 |
| 2010 | 12,658 |
| 2011 | 12,284 |
| 2012 | 12,412 |
| 2013 | 10,094 |
