| 2014 | 7,993 |
| 2015 | 8,423 |
| 2016 | 12,224[^cite_note-328] |

Casualty numbers have escalated significantly over time. According to a [Stratfor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratfor) report, the number of drug-related deaths in 2006 and 2007 (2,119 and 2,275) more than doubled to 5,207 in 2008. The number further increased substantially over the next two years, from 6,598 in 2009 to over 11,000 in 2010. According to data from the Mexican government, the death numbers are even higher: 9,616 in 2009, 15,273 in 2010, coming to a total of 47,515 killings between 2006 and January 2012.[^cite_note-stratfor-280][^cite_note-FObR0-329][^cite_note-iRygq-330]&#x20;

It is often not clear what deaths are part of the Mexican drug war versus general criminal [homicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homicides), and different sources give different estimates.[^cite_note-justicemexico-report-331] Casualties are often measured indirectly by estimated total deaths from organized [crime in Mexico](5182370.md).[^cite_note-justicemexico-report-331] This amounts to about 115,000 people in the years 2007–2018.[^cite_note-justicemexico-data-327] From 2018 to 2020, it was estimated that there were 11,400 reports of gang violence, and over 80% of the attacks targeted civilians, resulting in 13,000 related deaths during the period.[^cite_note-332]&#x20;

### Violence



Count of murders in Mexico's drug conflicts (December 2006 to December 2010)

Although Mexican authorities often distinguish between homicides linked to organized crime and those that are not, the conflict has strained state resources and created an environment of impunity that has worsened crime overall.[^cite_note-impunity1-270][^cite_note-impunity2-271] In 2009, the Mexican attorney general's office claimed that 9 of 10 victims of the Mexican drug war are members of organized crime groups,[^cite_note-Yqtc7-333] although other sources have questioned this figure.[^cite_note-EamJZ-334] Deaths among military and police personnel are an estimated 7% of the total.[^cite_note-5_myths-335] The states that suffer from the conflict the most are Baja California, [Guerrero](195816.md), [Chihuahua](23962301.md), Michoacán, Tamaulipas, [Nuevo León](222247.md), and Sinaloa.&#x20;



Total homicides in Mexico (1990–2023)

Cartels have used public displays of violence, including videos of executions and street banners, to spread fear and assert control.[^cite_note-Ha2qk-336] They have also committed [terrorist attacks](221350.md).[^cite_note-L8h6s-337] The 2008 [Morelia grenade attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morelia_grenade_attacks) killed eight and injured more than 100 civilians celebrating [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Mexico\)).[^cite_note-jbR4e-338] In 2011, Los Zetas carried out [an arson attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Monterrey_casino_attack) on the Casino Royale in [Monterrey](40455.md), killing 52 civilians.[^cite_note-339] Some see these efforts as intended to sap the morale of the Mexican government; others see them as an effort to let citizens know who is winning the war. Similarly, at least one dozen Mexican *[norteño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norte%C3%B1o_\(music\))* musicians have been murdered. Most of the victims performed *[narcocorridos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcocorridos)*, folk songs that tell the stories of the Mexican drug trade.[^cite_note-eja2W-340]&#x20;

On July 10, 2008, the Mexican government announced plans to nearly double the size of its [Federal Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Preventive_Police) force to reduce the role of the military in combating drug trafficking.&#x20;

### Effects on the economy

Beyond its social and political toll, the Mexican drug war has also had significant economic consequences. It has increased costs for businesses, disrupted supply chains, and raised insurance premiums.[^cite_note-341] [Small and medium businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium_enterprises_in_Mexico) in cartel-dominated areas are affected by widespread [extortion](162449.md), known as *piso*, while multinational corporations have delayed or reduced investments due to security risks.[^cite_note-342] At the government level, the costs of military deployment and police expansion have represented a growing fiscal burden. Given Mexico's high rates of [tax noncompliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_noncompliance), these expenditures have further constrained public finances and limited funding for other essential services.[^cite_note-343] [Finance Minister](8056561.md) [Agustín Carstens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agust%C3%ADn_Carstens) said that the deteriorating security alone is reducing gross domestic product annually by 1% in Mexico, Latin America's second-largest economy.[^cite_note-YpVA6-344]&#x20;

### Effects on human rights

See also: [Human rights in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Mexico)



A Mexican Army technical equipped with a [Mk 19 grenade launcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mk_19_grenade_launcher) at a [random checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Random_checkpoint)



[2011 Mexican protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Mexican_protests) against cartel violence and government [disregard](66220.md)



Protest camp on [Paseo de la Reforma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paseo_de_la_Reforma) with portraits of the 43 missing students from the [2014 Iguala mass kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Iguala_mass_kidnapping)

Since the beginning of the conflict, the military's role in civilian law enforcement has been a subject of controversy. Article 129 of the [Mexican Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Constitution) states that, in times of peace, the armed forces can only perform functions that are strictly connected to military discipline.[^cite_note-345] Calderón justified their deployment under his constitutional role as [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Mexican Armed Forces](23384265.md) (Article 89, Section VI), but this interpretation has been criticized for circumventing constitutional limits on military authority. Efforts to formalize this role, such as Peña Nieto's 2017 Internal Security Law, and López Obrador's 2022 transfer of the National Guard to SEDENA, were struck down by the [Supreme Court](2476388.md), though in practice the deployments have continued under executive decrees, and in the case of López Obrador, [constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_amendments).[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-hSAWH-347]&#x20;

Concentration of power in the executive branch, along with corruption in the legislature and judiciary, has been linked to the deterioration of Mexico's human rights situation. Problems include police abuses such as torture and threats, the autonomy of the military and its consequences, and the ineffectiveness of the judiciary in upholding and preserving human rights. Some forms of human rights violations by Mexican authorities include illegal arrests, secret and [indefinite detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_detention), [torture](47702.md), [rape](7980471.md), [extrajudicial execution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_execution), and [fabrication of evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_evidence).[^cite_note-rF215-348][^cite_note-Dx7dR-349][^cite_note-ykhZo-350] Federal agencies active in the conflict, such as the now-defunct [Federal Investigative Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Ministerial_Police) (AFI), were also criticized for abuses; in one case, a detainee died in custody, and an implicated agent later escaped while on bail.[^cite_note-ColleenCook-60][^cite_note-3HivI-351] The AFI, associated with multiple corruption and detainee torture cases, was declared a failure and disbanded in 2009.[^cite_note-1XQOt-352][^cite_note-8WfV8-353]&#x20;

Some groups are especially vulnerable to human rights abuses collateral to drug law enforcement. Specifically in northern border states that have seen elevated levels of drug-related violence, human rights violations of injection drug users (IDUs) and sex workers by law enforcement personnel include physical and sexual violence, extortion, and targeting for accessing or possession of injection equipment or practicing sex work, although these activities are legal.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-PolliniLozada2010-355][^cite_note-PolliniBrouwer2008-356] Ethnic prejudices have also emerged in the drug war, and indigenous communities have been targeted by the police, military, drug traffickers, and the justice system. According to the Mexican [National Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Human_Rights_Commission_\(Mexico\)), nearly one-third of the indigenous prisoners in Mexico in 2001 were in prison for federal crimes, which are mostly drug-related.[^cite_note-VOF8o-357] Such targeting is especially deleterious because members of these marginalized communities often lack the resources to enforce their rights.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-PolliniLozada2010-355][^cite_note-PolliniBrouwer2008-356]&#x20;

Another major concern is the lack of implementation of the [Leahy Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leahy_Law) in the U.S. and the consequences of that in worsening the human rights situation in Mexico. Critics have argued that U.S. assistance to Mexican security forces has conflicted with the Leahy Law, which prohibits aid to units implicated in human rights abuses, pointing to cases where trained units were later accused of violations.[^cite_note-358]&#x20;

### Effects on public health

> "**The social fabric is so destroyed that it cannot be healed in one generation or two because wounds become deeply embedded...Mexico has a humanitarian tragedy and we have not grasped how big it is.**"—*Elena Azaola, Centre for Social Anthropology High Studies and Research*[^cite_note-E2T7a-359]&#x20;

Illicit drug use in Mexico is low compared to the United States, but it is on the rise, with the availability of narcotics gradually increasing since the 1980s.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-NIH-361] The export rate of cocaine to the US decreased following stricter [border control](247927.md) measures in response to the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-NIH-361][^cite_note-Hgnf8-362][^cite_note-7x6lB-363] Drug shipments are often delayed in Mexican border towns before delivery to the U.S., which has forced drug traffickers to increase prices to account for transportation costs. These delays have contributed to the increased rates of local drug consumption.[^cite_note-NIH-361] As a result of "spillover" along the U.S.-bound drug trafficking routes and more stringent border enforcement, Mexico's northern border states have seen increased levels of drug consumption and abuse, including elevated rates of drug injection, up to 10 to 15 times the national average.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-BucardoBrouwer2005-364][^cite_note-RamosFerreira-Pinto2009-365]&#x20;

These rates are accompanied by mounting rates of HIV and STIs among injection drug users (IDUs) and sex workers, reaching a 5.5% prevalence in cities such as Tijuana and Ciudad Juárez, which also report STI rates of 64% and 83%, respectively.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354] Violence and extortion of IDUs and sex workers directly and indirectly elevate the levels of risk behavior and poor health outcomes among members of these groups.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-leCgF-366] Marginalization of these vulnerable groups by way of physical and sexual violence and extortion by police threatens the cross-over of infection from high-prevalence groups to the general population.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-WerbWood2008-367][^cite_note-LiuGrusky2006-368] In particular, decreased access to public health services such as syringe exchange programs and confiscation of syringes can precipitate a cascade of health harms.[^cite_note-BeletskyLozada2012-369][^cite_note-MyerStrathdee2011-370][^cite_note-HayashiTi2013-371] Geographic diffusion of epidemics from the northern border states elsewhere is also possible with the rotation of police and military personnel stationed in drug conflict areas with high infection prevalence.[^cite_note-BeletskyMartinez2012-354][^cite_note-WerbWood2008-367][^cite_note-LiuGrusky2006-368] With increased drug use, there has been a parallel rise in demand for drug user treatment in Mexico.[^cite_note-NIH-361]&#x20;

### Effects on Women

Main article: [Women in the Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Mexican_drug_war)



An [anti-monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-monuments_in_Mexico) against [femicides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femicides_in_Mexico) in Mexico, in front of the [Palacio de Bellas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Bellas_Artes) in Mexico City

[Women in the Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Mexican_drug_war) have been participants and civilians. They have served for or been harmed by all belligerents. There have been female combatants in the military, police, cartels, and gangs.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] Women officials, judges, prosecutors, lawyers, paralegals,[^cite_note-374] reporters, business owners, social media influencers, teachers, and non-governmental organizations directors and workers have also been involved in different capacities.[^cite_note-375] Women citizens and foreigners, including migrants,[^cite_note-376] have been raped,[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] tortured,[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] and murdered in the conflict.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383][^cite_note-\:5-384][^cite_note-385]&#x20;

Cartels and gangs fighting in the conflict carry out [sex trafficking in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_trafficking_in_Mexico) as an alternative source of profits.[^cite_note-world.time.com-386][^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] Some members of criminal organizations also abduct women and girls for [sexual slavery](173634.md)[^cite_note-world.time.com-386] and carry out [sexual assault of migrants from Latin America to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_assault_of_migrants_from_Latin_America_to_the_United_States).[^cite_note-390]&#x20;

Groups of women known as *[madres buscadoras](77099430.md)* ('searching mothers') have become prominent for organizing searches for disappeared relatives, and their work has uncovered unmarked [mass graves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_graves) that provide evidence of widespread disappearances.[^cite_note-391]&#x20;

### Journalists and the media

Main article: [List of journalists and media workers killed in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_journalists_and_media_workers_killed_in_Mexico)



Demonstration against the murder of Mexican journalist [Javier Valdez Cárdenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_Valdez_C%C3%A1rdenas) in May 2017

The increase in violence related with organized crime has significantly deteriorated the conditions in which local journalism is practiced.[^cite_note-XveMh-392] In the first years of the 21st century, Mexico was considered the most dangerous country to practice journalism, according to groups like the National Human Rights Commission, [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), and the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists). Between 2000 and 2012, several dozen journalists, including [Miguel Ángel López Velasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_%C3%81ngel_L%C3%B3pez_Velasco), [Luis Carlos Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Carlos_Santiago), and [Valentín Valdés Espinosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valent%C3%ADn_Vald%C3%A9s_Espinosa), were murdered there for covering the Mexican drug war.[^cite_note-ESYbS-393][^cite_note-tuckman1-394][^cite_note-velasco1-395]&#x20;

The offices of [Televisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisa) and local newspapers have been bombed.[^cite_note-d6Zpl-396] Cartels have also threatened to kill news reporters in the U.S. who have done coverage on the drug violence.[^cite_note-YsEiN-397] Some media networks stopped reporting on drug crimes, while others have been infiltrated by cartels.[^cite_note-lK9Gq-398][^cite_note-8zwbY-399] Since harassment neutralized many traditional media outlets, anonymous, sensationalized blogs like [Blog del Narco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blog_del_Narco) took on the role of reporting on events related to the drug war.[^cite_note-Tuckman-400] Cartels responded by targeting bloggers and citizen journalists active on social media.[^cite_note-mE6k0-401] Several have been tortured or killed for posting and denouncing cartel activities. In September 2011, citizen journalist NenaDLaredo of the website Nuevo Laredo Envivo was allegedly murdered by Los Zetas.[^cite_note-p8K1T-402]&#x20;

In May 2012, several journalist murders occurred in [Veracruz](63125.md). [Regina Martinez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regina_Martinez) of *[Proceso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proceso_\(magazine\))* was murdered in [Xalapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xalapa). A few days later, three Veracruz [photojournalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photojournalists) were tortured and killed, and their dismembered bodies were dumped in a canal. The targeting of journalists has prompted national and international condemnation. International watchdogs have urged Mexican authorities to end impunity for attacks on journalists.[^cite_note-tuckman1-394][^cite_note-zablu1-403][^cite_note-WKCEO-404]&#x20;

About 74 percent of the journalists killed since 1992 in Mexico have been reporters for print newspapers, followed in number by Internet media and radio at about 11 percent each. Television journalism accounts for 4 percent of the deaths.[^cite_note-Lx1ow-405][^cite_note-nCzTN-406][^cite_note-MXJBJ-407]&#x20;

### Murders of politicians

Main article: [List of politicians killed in the Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_politicians_killed_in_the_Mexican_drug_war)

Since the start of the Mexican drug war in 2006, drug trafficking organizations have increasingly targeted politicians, especially local leaders in contested areas.[^cite_note-mayorstarget-408] Part of the strategy used by criminal groups behind the killings of local figures is the weakening of the [local governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government).[^cite_note-mayorstarget-408] For example, [María Santos Gorrostieta Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%ADa_Santos_Gorrostieta_Salazar), former mayor of [Tiquicheo, Michoacán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiquicheo), who had survived three earlier assassination attempts and the murder of her husband, was abducted and beaten to death in November 2012.[^cite_note-22LFM-409] Extreme violence puts politicians at the mercy of cartels, allowing them to increase their control of government structures and expand their influence.[^cite_note-mayorstarget-408] The [2018 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Mexican_general_election) were the deadliest on record, with more than 130 candidates and politicians killed nationwide. Political violence has been most acute in states such as Guerrero, Michoacán, Veracruz, Guanajuato, and Oaxaca, where local governance is highly fragmented.[^cite_note-410]&#x20;

In addition, because mayors usually appoint local [police chiefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_police), they are seen by cartels as key assets in their criminal activities to control the police forces in their areas of influence.[^cite_note-local-411] Cartels also seek to control the local governments to win government contracts and concessions; these projects help them ingrain themselves in the community and gain the loyalty and respect of the communities in which they operate.[^cite_note-local-411] Politicians are usually targeted for three reasons: (1) Political figures who are honest pose a direct threat to organized crime, and are consequently killed by cartels; (2) Politicians make arrangements to protect a certain cartel and are killed by a rival cartel; and (3) A cartel kills politicians to heat the turf of the rival cartel that operates in the area.[^cite_note-5rpLG-412]&#x20;

### Massacres and exploitation of migrants

See also: [2010 San Fernando massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_San_Fernando_massacre); [2011 San Fernando massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_San_Fernando_massacre); and [Allende, Coahuila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allende,_Coahuila)

Cartels have engaged in kidnapping, ransom, murder, robbery, and extortion of [migrants traveling from Central America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_migrant_caravans) through Mexico on their way to the United States and Canada. Cartels have also forced migrants to join their organization and work for them, a situation that has been described as [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery).[^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] [Mass graves](1349803.md) have been also discovered in Mexico containing bodies of migrants.[^cite_note-GW3H2-415] In 2011, 177 bodies were found in a mass grave in [San Fernando, Tamaulipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Fernando,_Tamaulipas), the same area where the bodies of 72 migrants were found in 2010,[^cite_note-kuNNC-416] where most victims "died of blunt force trauma to the head."[^cite_note-\:5-384]&#x20;

Cartels have also infiltrated the Mexican government's immigration agencies and attacked and threatened immigration officers.[^cite_note-pressly-417] The [National Human Rights Commission of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Human_Rights_Commission_\(Mexico\)) (Comisión Nacional de los Derechos Humanos, CNDH) said that 11,000 migrants had been kidnapped in 6 months in 2010 by drug cartels.[^cite_note-EWRxE-418]&#x20;

### Human trafficking

See also: [Human trafficking in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_trafficking_in_Mexico)

There are documented links between Mexican cartels and [human trafficking](19333662.md) for forced labor, forced prostitution, and rape. The wife of a cartel leader described a system in which young girls became prostitutes and then were forced to work in drug factories.[^cite_note-lcacho-419] In the early 2010s, Los Zetas reportedly began to move into the prostitution business (including the prostitution of children) after previously only supplying women to already existing networks.[^cite_note-aMCXa-420]&#x20;

The U.S. State Department says that the practice of [forced labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labor) in Mexico is larger in extent than [forced prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_prostitution).[^cite_note-3occ7-421] Mexican journalists like [Lydia Cacho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia_Cacho) have been threatened and forced into exile for reporting on these events.[^cite_note-422]&#x20;

## Effects internationally

### Canada

The Mexican Army curtailed the ability of the Mexican drug cartels to move cocaine inside the U.S. and Canada, prompting an upsurge in [gang violence in Vancouver in 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Vancouver_gang_war), where the cocaine price has increased from \$23,300 to almost \$39,000 per kilo as the Canadian drug markets experienced prolonged shortages.[^cite_note-progress-65] As evidence of this pressure, the U.S. government stated the amount of cocaine seized on U.S. soil dropped by 41 percent between early 2007 and mid-2008.[^cite_note-progress-65] Since 2009, Vancouver has become the Mexican cartels' main center of operations in Canada.[^cite_note-4k68d-423]&#x20;

### Europe

The U.S. market is being eclipsed by booming demand for cocaine in Europe, where users now pay twice the going U.S. rate.[^cite_note-progress-65] In 2008, U.S. Attorney General [Michael Mukasey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Mukasey) announced that an international drug interdiction operation, [Project Reckoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Solare), involving law enforcement in the United States, Italy, Canada, Mexico and Guatemala had netted more than 500 organized crime members involved in the cocaine trade. The announcement highlighted the Italian-Mexican cocaine connection.[^cite_note-Stratfor_Intelligence-86]&#x20;

Concerns about European security and the trafficking of drugs through the European continent have grown in recent years, and, in December 2022, [Europol](525329.md) and the DEA released a joint report on the situation involving Mexican drug trafficking through the EU.[^cite_note-424]&#x20;

In December 2011, the government of Spain remarked that Mexican cartels had multiplied their operations in that country, becoming the main entry point of cocaine into Europe.[^cite_note-FATlW-425] In 2012, it was reported that Mexican cartels had joined forces with the [Sicilian Mafia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sicilian_Mafia), when Italian officials unearthed information that [Palermo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palermo)'s black market, along with other Italian ports, was used by Mexico's drug cartels as a conduit to bring drugs to the European market, in which they had been trafficking drugs, particularly cocaine, throughout the Atlantic Ocean for over ten years to Europe.[^cite_note-BsChX-426]&#x20;

In 2016, investigations into transatlantic drug trafficking revealed that the [Kinahan Clan](71464023.md), Ireland's largest drug traffickers, had joined with prominent figures in Mexico, South America, West Africa, and Europe to form an informal "Super Cartel." However, the extent of its coordination remains unclear.[^cite_note-\:1-427] The 2017 wedding of [Daniel Kinahan](64256824.md) in Dubai helped investigators identify key members such as [Ridouan Taghi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridouan_Taghi), [Ricardo Riquelme Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Riquelme_Vega), [Naoufal Fassih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naoufal_Fassih), and Camorra boss [Raffaele Imperiale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raffaele_Imperiale).[^cite_note-\:2-428] In 2022–23, a major international operation led to 49 arrests, including traffickers Edin "Tito" Gacanin and Zuhair Belkhair, accused of moving large amounts of cocaine through Rotterdam; though many members of the "Super Cartel" remain in custody, both were released shortly after their arrests.&#x20;

#### Involvement of Mexican cartels in the Russo-Ukrainian War

In July 2025, the [Mexican Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Centre_\(Mexico\)) in collaboration with Ukrainian security forces, revealed that in the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) Colombian and Mexican mercenaries were sent in the war-zone in the [International Legion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Legion_\(Ukraine\)) by the drug cartels to gain war tactics and especially knowledge on the use of drones FPV for battles with the security forces and rival cartels in Mexico.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] In August 2025, it was revealed that the [Jalisco New Generation Cartel](35006872.md) (CJNG) created a specific paramilitary unit, of at least 10 members, for the use of drones FPV using the tactics learned in the War in Ukraine.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]&#x20;

### Guatemala

The Mexican Army crackdown has driven some cartels to seek a safer location for their operations across the border in Guatemala, attracted by corruption, weak policing, and its position on the overland smuggling route.[^cite_note-b3kxI-435][^cite_note-Miglierini-436] The smugglers pick up drugs from small planes that land at private airstrips hidden in the Guatemalan jungle. The cargo is then moved up through Mexico to the U.S. border. Guatemala has also arrested dozens of drug suspects and torched huge cannabis and poppy fields. The U.S. government sent speedboats and night-vision goggles under a regional drug aid package.[^cite_note-437]&#x20;

Los Zetas have gained ground in Guatemala after they killed several high-profile members and the supreme leader of *Los Leones*, an organized crime group from Guatemala.[^cite_note-iqHM7-438] In February 2009, Los Zetas threatened to kill the president of Guatemala, [Álvaro Colom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%81lvaro_Colom).[^cite_note-Ej8Al-439] On March 1, 2010, Guatemala's chief of national police and the country's top anti-drugs official were arrested over alleged links to drug trafficking.[^cite_note-Miglierini-436] A report from the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution)[^cite_note-4B9ky-440] warns that, without proactive, timely efforts, the violence will spread throughout the Central American region.[^cite_note-TFAuM-441][^cite_note-FWJF8-442] In August 2025, Guatemala granted temporary [humanitarian status](50128538.md) to 161 Mexicans fleeing cartel violence in Chiapas.[^cite_note-443]&#x20;

### South America

[Patricio Pazmiño](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Patricio_Pazmi%C3%B1o\&action=edit\&redlink=1), the Interior Minister of Ecuador, stated that the [February 2021 riots at three prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2021_Ecuadorian_prison_riots) that took 79 lives were related to Mexican and Colombian drug gangs. The government intercepted a record 126 tons of cocaine in 2020.[^cite_note-444]&#x20;

On September 8, 2021, National Prosecutor [Jorge Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Abbott) declared that Mexican cartels were attempting to establish themselves in Chile.[^cite_note-DWdrug-445] It is known that [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md) has attempted to use Chile as a transit route for the shipment of cocaine to [Rotterdam](26049.md) in the Netherlands.[^cite_note-DWdrug-445] The activity of the [CJNG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CJNG) includes an attempt at establishing a drug laboratory in [Iquique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iquique) as well as the import of marihuana through the port of [San Antonio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio,_Chile).[^cite_note-DWdrug-445]&#x20;

### United States

See also: [Mérida Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida_Initiative), [ATF gunwalking scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATF_gunwalking_scandal), and [Opioid epidemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic_in_the_United_States)



[Special reaction team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_reaction_team) (SRT) during operation against the [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md) in the [Arizona](21883824.md)'s western desert, October 2011



[Joaquin Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaqu%C3%ADn_%22El_Chapo%22_Guzm%C3%A1n) in U.S. custody when extradited on January 19, 2017

The [U.S. Justice Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Justice_Department) considers the Mexican drug cartels to be the "greatest organized crime threat to the United States."[^cite_note-QLoLv-446] In seeking partnership from the United States, Mexican officials point out that the illicit drug trade is a shared problem in need of a shared solution, and remark that most of the financing for the Mexican traffickers comes from American drug consumers.[^cite_note-l1vpn-447] On March 25, 2009, U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) stated that "\[America's] insatiable demand for illegal drugs fuels the drug trade", and that "the United States bears shared responsibility for the drug-fueled violence sweeping Mexico."[^cite_note-R7eot-448]&#x20;

U.S. State Department officials knew that Mexican ex-president Felipe Calderón's willingness to work with the United States was unprecedented on issues of security, crime and drugs, so the [U.S. Congress](31756.md) passed legislation in late June 2008 to provide Mexico and Central American countries with US\$1.6 billion for the [Mérida Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9rida_Initiative), a three-year international assistance plan that provides law enforcement training and equipment, as well as technical advice to strengthen the national justice systems. Under the [Foreign Narcotics Kingpin Designation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Narcotics_Kingpin_Designation_Act), the United States has sanctioned numerous Mexican drug traffickers and organizations by freezing assets and prohibiting financial transactions with them.&#x20;

Currently, the Mexican drug cartels already have a presence in most major U.S. cities.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-449] In 2009, the Justice Department reported that Mexican drug cartels distribute drugs in nearly 200 cities across the United States,[^cite_note-PzJlc-450] including Los Angeles, Chicago, and Atlanta.[^cite_note-baIAz-451] Gang-related activity and violence has increased along the U.S. Southwest [border region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border), as U.S.-based [gangs](80408.md) act as enforcers for Mexican drug cartels.[^cite_note-58reV-452] In October 2025, Mexican cartels are issuing bounties up to \$50,000 for a hit on ICE and CBP agents according to the Department of Homeland Security.&#x20;

#### U.S. death toll and national security



This [ICE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Immigration_and_Customs_Enforcement) photo shows people under arrest. Officials announced the discovery of a large drug trafficking operation from Mexico into [Arizona](21883824.md).

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(November 2020)* |
| --- | --- |

U.S. authorities reported a spike in killings, kidnappings, and home invasions connected to Mexican cartels, and at least 19 Americans were killed in 2008.[^cite_note-3yiyD-453][^cite_note-LLjNe-454] Another 92 Americans were killed between June 2009 and June 2010.[^cite_note-zgr2r-455]&#x20;

The [U.S. Joint Forces Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Joint_Forces_Command) noted in a December 2008 report that the conflict will have a major impact on the stability of the Mexican state over the next several years, and therefore would demand an American response based on the implications for homeland security alone.[^cite_note-joint-456] After the JFC broached this issue in its 2008 report, several journalists and academics have discussed the possibility that Mexico could become a [failed state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_state).[^cite_note-0X764-457][^cite_note-uAi5v-458][^cite_note-eAwl6-459][^cite_note-n1PpO-460] The Mexican government responded negatively to the U.S. government raising the prospect of Mexico becoming a failed state.[^cite_note-rieff-461][^cite_note-CdWTD-462] To smooth over relations with Mexico over this issue, Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) personally visited Mexico City in March 2009, followed by a visit by President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) a month later.[^cite_note-rieff-461]&#x20;



Number of yearly U.S. [opioid overdose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_overdose) deaths from all opioid drugs.

[^cite_note-463]

In March 2009, the U.S. DHS said that it was considering using the National Guard to counter the threat of drug violence in Mexico from spreading to the U.S. The governors of Arizona and Texas have asked the federal government to send additional National Guard troops to help those already there supporting local law enforcement efforts against drug trafficking.[^cite_note-BBC_News_–_Americas_–_March-12-09-110] Calls for National Guard deployment on the border greatly increased after the 2010 murder of Arizona rancher [Robert Krentz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Krentz), possibly at the hands of Mexican drug smugglers.[^cite_note-ixl1t-464][^cite_note-SVHerald_NothingNew-465]&#x20;

In March 2009, the Obama administration outlined plans to redeploy more than 500 federal agents to border posts and redirect \$200 million to combat smuggling of illegal drugs, money, and weapons.[^cite_note-I9FvA-466] On May 25, 2010, President Obama authorized deployment of 1,200 National Guard troops to the U.S. border with Mexico to assist with border protection and enforcement activities, as well as help train additional Customs and Border Protection agents.[^cite_note-xcvZn-467] The [Washington Office on Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Office_on_Latin_America) said the U.S. southwest border region remained calm, with a homicide rate lower than the national average.[^cite_note-8Rfrd-468][^cite_note-xC3RE-469]&#x20;

In 2021, around 80,411 people died from [opioid overdoses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_overdose) in the United States.[^cite_note-470] Many of the deaths are from an extremely potent opioid, [fentanyl](141915.md), which is trafficked from Mexico.[^cite_note-471] The drug's precursor chemicals, which have a variety of legitimate uses, are manufactured in China, then shipped to Mexico, where it is processed and packaged, which is then smuggled into the US by drug cartels.[^cite_note-472] The [opioid crisis in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic_in_the_United_States) is largely fueled by drugs smuggled from Mexico; approximately 98% of fentanyl entering the U.S. comes from Mexico.[^cite_note-473] In 2023, the Biden administration announced a crackdown on members of the [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md) smuggling fentanyl into the United States.[^cite_note-474] In 2025, President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) launched a process to designate Mexican drug cartels and other criminal organizations as [foreign terrorist organizations](421835.md).[^cite_note-\:25-475] The Trump administration has considered [drone strikes](38212231.md) against cartels in Mexico.[^cite_note-476]&#x20;

### West Africa

At least nine Mexican and Colombian drug cartels have established bases in several West African nations, with notable activity in [Guinea-Bissau](12186.md) and [Sierra Leone](27308.md), among other places.[^cite_note-Brice-477] They have reportedly worked closely with local criminal gangs to carve out a staging area for access to the lucrative European market. The Colombian and Mexican cartels have discovered that it is easier to smuggle large loads into West Africa and then break that up into smaller shipments to Europe – mostly Spain, the United Kingdom and France.[^cite_note-Brice-477] Higher demand for cocaine in Western Europe in addition to North American interdiction campaigns has led to dramatically increased trafficking in the region: nearly 50% of all non-U.S. bound cocaine, or about 13% of all global flows, is now smuggled through West Africa.[^cite_note-pMywC-478]&#x20;

## Controversies

### Allegations of CIA involvement

See also: [Allegations of CIA drug trafficking § Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_CIA_drug_trafficking#Mexico)



[Jesús Vicente Zambada Niebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Vicente_Zambada_Niebla) is the son of [Ismael Zambada García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismael_Zambada_Garc%C3%ADa) (alias, "El Mayo"), one of the top leaders of the Sinaloa drug-trafficking organization

[Vicente Zambada Niebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Zambada_Niebla), a high-ranking member of the Sinaloa Cartel, claimed after his arrest that he and other Sinaloa Cartel members had received immunity from U.S. agents and a virtual license to smuggle cocaine over the United States border, in exchange for intelligence about rival cartels engaged in the Mexican drug war.[^cite_note-Narcosphere.narconews.com-479][^cite_note-Name_*-480]&#x20;

In October 2013, two former federal agents and an ex-CIA contractor told an American television network that CIA operatives, including [Félix Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Rodr%C3%ADguez_\(soldier\)), were involved in the kidnapping and murder of DEA covert agent Enrique Camarena, because he was a threat to the agency's drug operations in Mexico. According to them, the CIA was collaborating with drug traffickers, and using its share of the profits to finance Nicaraguan Contra rebels attempting to overthrow Nicaragua's [Sandinista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinista_National_Liberation_Front) government. A CIA spokesman responded, calling it "ridiculous" to suggest that the Agency had anything to do with the murder of a U.S. federal agent or the escape of his alleged killer.[^cite_note-Wm1Xh-481]&#x20;

### Operation Trizor and Condor

In a joint operation funded by the United States helicopters were used to spray marijuana plants with an herbicide called paraquat. The operations eliminated 22,000 acres of poppies and 9,500 acres of marijuana fields.[^cite_note-\:6-482] Of over 2,000 samples sent to the PharmaChem Foundation 22% of them were found to have been contaminated with paraquat.[^cite_note-483] Laboratories in the U.S. at the time of operations' publication were overwhelmed with requests to test marijuana. The effects were estimated to potentially effect 13 million people.[^cite_note-\:6-482] Due to its link to Parkinson's, pulmonary fibrosis and other diseases, the European Union banned the herbicide in 2007, followed by China in 2016.[^cite_note-484]&#x20;

### Money laundering

Although Mexican drug cartels and their Colombian suppliers generate and launder between \$18 billion to \$39 billion from sales to the United States each year,[^cite_note-Booth-485] the U.S. and Mexican governments have been criticized for their unwillingness or slow response to confront the various cartels' financial operations, including [money laundering](19390.md).[^cite_note-Booth-485][^cite_note-1jiOB-486][^cite_note-LA_Times-487] The U.S. DEA has identified the need to increase financial investigations relating to the movement of illegal drug funds to Mexico.[^cite_note-Laundering-488][^cite_note-tev9e-489] The DEA has noted that the U.S. and Mexican financial services industry continues to be a facilitator for money laundering.[^cite_note-Laundering-488][^cite_note-U3Fph-490]&#x20;

In August 2010, President Felipe Calderón proposed new measures to combat cash smuggling and money laundering. Calderón proposed a ban on cash purchases of real estate and of certain luxury goods that cost more than 100,000 pesos (about US\$8,104). His package would also require more businesses to report large transactions, such as real estate, jewelry and purchases of [armor plating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armor_plating).[^cite_note-LA_Times-487] In June 2010, Calderón announced strict limits on the amount in U.S. dollars that can be deposited or exchanged in banks,[^cite_note-LA_Times-487] but the restrictions were relaxed in 2014.[^cite_note-Booth-485][^cite_note-491]&#x20;

In 2011, [Wachovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wachovia), at one time a major U.S. bank, was implicated in laundering money for Mexican drug traffickers. In a settlement, Wachovia paid federal authorities \$110 million in forfeiture.[^cite_note-legpS-492] A [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) report[^cite_note-afJdM-493][^cite_note-nIHhE-494] from the permanent subcommittee for investigations revealed in July 2012 that [HSBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSBC) moved \$7 billion in bulk cash from Mexico to the U.S., most of it suspected to assist Mexican cartels and U.S. drug cartels in moving money to the U.S.[^cite_note-YrogN-495][^cite_note-fine-496] While regulators have flagged money laundering problems at HSBC for nearly a decade, the bank continued to avoid compliance. In December 2012, HSBC settled for a \$1.93 billion fine.[^cite_note-oJoPW-497]&#x20;

### Drug demand and decriminalization

According to former Presidents [Fernando Henrique Cardoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Henrique_Cardoso) of Brazil, [Ernesto Zedillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Zedillo) of Mexico and [César Gaviria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%A9sar_Gaviria) of Colombia, the United States-led drug war is pushing Latin America into a downward spiral; Cardoso said in a conference that "the available evidence indicates that the war on drugs is a failed war".[^cite_note-Hjefu-498] The panel of the Latin American Commission on Drugs and Democracy commission, headed by Cardoso, stated that the countries involved in this war should remove the "taboos" and re-examine the anti-drug programs. Latin American governments have followed the advice of the U.S. to combat the drug war, but the policies have had little effect. The commission made some recommendations to United States President Barack Obama to consider new policies, such as [decriminalization of marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decriminalization_of_non-medical_cannabis_in_the_United_States) and to treat drug use as a public health problem and not as a security problem.[^cite_note-k86cw-499] The [Council on Hemispheric Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Hemispheric_Affairs) states it is time to consider [drug decriminalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_decriminalization) and [legalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_legalization).[^cite_note-4B958-500]&#x20;

[RAND](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAND) studies released in the mid-1990s found that using drug user treatment to reduce drug consumption in the United States is seven times more cost-effective than law enforcement efforts alone, and it could potentially cut consumption by a third.[^cite_note-cMAZA-501] In 2011, the Obama administration requested approximately \$5.6 billion to support demand reduction. This includes a 13% increase for prevention and almost a 4% increase for treatment. The overall 2011 counter-drug request for supply reduction and domestic law enforcement is \$15.5 billion, with \$521.1 million in new funding.[^cite_note-n52q6-502]&#x20;

In April 2020, the [Mexican Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Senate) approved an amnesty law for first-time, nonviolent offenders, including those convicted of small-scale drug possession.[^cite_note-503] In June 2021, the Supreme Court [decriminalized recreational cannabis use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Mexico), a decision viewed as a step toward modernizing national drug policy. However, critics note that these measures have had little impact on drug-related violence, since marijuana sales in Mexico constitute only a minor share of cartel revenues compared to synthetic drugs, cocaine, and other illicit activities.[^cite_note-504]&#x20;
